ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e219	One of the headlines of the next year’s budget debate on energy and the environment could be ‘Global heat — local cold’. Those of us who participated in the Cancún climate summit have to state that despite a cold fall here in Norway, global warming is continuing, with a risk of great consequences for people world over and for the lack of nature. Therefore, it is – rightly – expected that Norway will actively contribute both internationally and nationally to reducing greenhouse gas emissions and ensuring natural deficiency. The budget proposal from the government for the next year leads to an additional strengthening on both of these two important areas. One of the most important tools to meet the challenges of both global heat and local cold is the increase in renewable energy investment. The government suggests that one through Enova should also spend close to 2 billion in 2011. on measures to increase the production of renewable energy and heat, to drive energy efficiency improvements within construction and industry and to contribute to new renewable technologies. Here's the bet on the ocean wind one of the copies. The Green Certificate Agreement, signed last Wednesday, will contribute to 26.4 TWh of new energy, with the aim of ensuring an equal distribution of this between Norway and Sweden. It is a particularly important energy and climate action, and I would like to congratulate the Council of State and the Government that they have put this in place. The strengthening of state-owned State power also shows the possibility, through state ownership, of building up under renewable efforts both in Norway and beyond its borders. For the Centre Party, it is important to stress that the green certificates do not have to mean a weakening of State efforts in the field of renewable and energy efficiency. There is still a large unsolved potential for energy efficiency in industry and in construction, whether it is commercial or private. Research efforts on immature renewable energy technologies are becoming an increasingly important field of action in the future. Therefore, there is no need to fall asleep in laurel berries, but rather to focus heavily on what is both good climate policy and good industrial policy, as it can make significant contributions to green jobs throughout the country over the years to come. The need for more power generation and increased security of supply has become even more evident in this fall. Some parts of the country have experienced higher power prices than others, and the power grid to bind our country together is not sufficiently developed. Although governments have taken important steps to realize important transmission lines and build new energy, there are still major challenges. It is therefore important to stress that efforts to secure new transmission capacity in the West and North Norway must continue. The budget contributed funds to smoothing out the costs of people and businesses for renting on the Internet. However, there is reason to point out that these differences in costs still vary considerably, and that this must be an important priority to do something about. Energy policy will be a particularly important area for the future, both for the climate and to ensure people and businesses secure access to power at stable prices. When important posts in the later period have come into place, the time has come to look ahead. It is therefore gratifying that the Council of State is now putting down a public selection to follow up with a message about the long lines of energy policy. The budget, which receives multiple figures here today, also contains an important clarification that has been requested for a long time, namely a minimum rate for service purchases that SNO has with mountain government and mountain services. Such a financial plan is important in order to ensure competence, to ensure workers in these services and to be able to carry out a decent activity. The management of the protection area will become more and more important in the future, as the focus is moving from new protection to the follow-up of existing areas. The new management board is now in the start-up phase, and it is important to ensure these real management of the areas, as the idea was, and follow-up with resources channelled through this new structure. Finally, allow me to thank my colleagues in the Committee and the Committee Secretary for the orderly and sound management of the budget – a good process in the Committee. I also need to be able to address the distributed amendment, which a joint committee is behind.

It needed the world, and now it's important that the emissions are delivered. Norway provides internationally important contributions through rainforest management and CCS. At the same time, we will deliver national emission reductions in line with the climate agreement. The climate message will come next year as a direct follow-up to the enormous work done through Climate Change, and that is good. But I am particularly pleased that a few days ago it was announced that we have come a considerable step closer to one of the most important points of the climate agreement, namely green certificates. A total of 26.4 TWh new renewable energy is added in Norway and Sweden. There is a major step forward for the renewable initiative in Norway. It will significantly strengthen the power situation in Norway and is also a very good industrial policy. There are great opportunities for the supplier industry in this. The setting presented here continues the government's strong commitment to renewable energy, the development of the petroleum sector, the development of network connections and energy efficiency. Many important clarifications about future energy policies are now being made, with green certificates, with the Renewable Directive, the Marine Winds Act and large and important network deployments. I therefore believe it is right and important that the government now does down a public sample to look at the power balance in Norway, the relationship between exports and imports, foreign exchange etc. in the long term – 20–30–40 years in advance. There's a debate that this nation needs, and we're going to take it. There are many major and important environmental challenges. Of all these, the government chooses to focus on conservation of natural diversity and to strengthen environmental efforts in the northern region of this budget. I myself would like to mention in particular the addition that has been made to open-air life through the treatment in Stortinget. It's an area that deserves a lot of attention in the years that are coming, because the gains from the funds that we give to this are so huge. This winter, as last year, has so far been marked by much discussion about the high power prices we have seen in Norway over the last few weeks. In particular, there has been a focus on power prices in central Norway, which over time has had a higher electricity price than the rest of the country. The power of the East and South Western countries is shut down within a lack of transfer capacity northwards, which has led to a lower price here – not only than in central Norway, but in the rest of the Nordic market. We had a special situation in the power sector last year. We also have this year, with a very dry harvest and abnormally low temperatures. So far this year we have used more power in Norway than we have produced. At the same time, we're stronger than last year, and it's due to a number of relationships. Nuclear power is much more produced in Sweden than at the same time last year. Two new power areas were established in Norway during the winter 2009–2010. It allows for specific measures and specific information to the market within each electricity spot area as appropriate. Before Easter this year, Svenska Kraftnet carried out the final work on the Nea–Järpstrom line between Mid-Norway and Sweden. That action has been taken to significantly improve the power situation and transmission capacity into the area. Then, in addition to network investments, Statnet has put in place measures to secure the power supply in exposed areas before new lines are in place – energy options in consumption, objections to the linking of new consumption and increased preparedness to correct transmission system errors. There is no quick fix on the situation in the power sector. Throughout Norway, not just in central Norway, the solution to the challenges we see is that we produce more electricity and build more power lines. The budget presented here maintains and strengthens that development. All the parties on the Stortinget, except the Advance Party, have come together to say that solving the climate challenge is one of the most important things we face. We have agreed on a number of measures to reduce our emissions. This means that the parties at Stortinget have said that gas power without purification and other fossil energies does not have a place in the future Norwegian energy system. The government has announced that it will come back to Stortinget with a case about Mongstad, and that gives us the opportunity to take the big Mongstad debate. Mongstad has become more expensive and more expensive. We are now in the planning phase of the project. We discuss the bill on the project when the financing decision comes to Stortinget. In addition, as the Storting is aware, questions have been raised about the health risks associated with the use of amines. But I'm convinced that here we need to exercise patience, because the world is not going to achieve the 2 degree goal without the bridge CCS represents.

We need to develop environmental technologies and find solutions that others can use. Such a conversion is demanding, but it offers new opportunities. In a world where environmental problems become clearer, we want environmental companies to become the winners of tomorrow. In the government budget, the government writes that the main instruments of Norway’s climate policy are taxes and negotiable allowances. The problem for the government is that it is not willing to use the charges to reduce emissions. Norway needs to develop and implement new climate technologies and change lifestyles in order to reduce greenhouse gas emissions significantly, while maintaining the level of welfare. Norway must go from being a fossil nation to becoming a renewable nation. The left is pleased that we finally have an agreement on a scheme of green certificates, but we would like to stress that we also need an increased general effort to get more clean energy production in Norway. The left therefore proposes, inter alia: increasing direct grants to the Energy Fund, establishing a scheme of grants for the development of thermal energy, and we will give money to the MET Centre to speed up the development of marine wind turbines. This, in addition to a stronger commitment to environmentally friendly transport, is some of the priorities we have in our alternative state budget. The left is concerned about the progress of the government's moon landing in Mongstad, while the oil and energy minister seems to have forgotten the promise of Soria Moria to clean Kårstø. The government promised in the Soria Moria declaration to clean the gas power plant at Kårstø as soon as possible. This promise has not been fulfilled. The gas power plant and the gas terminal at Kårstø emit more GHG together than any other GHG source in Norway. Oil and energy minister Terje Riis-Johansen, in his press release on the State budget, says that man-made climate change is our biggest challenge, and that CO2 capture and storage must become an important tool to cut greenhouse gas emissions. But in the proposed state budget, it seems that both Kårstø and Mongstad have unfortunately been allocated significant amounts of free allowances. In our view, such subsidisation of emissions is totally wrong and directly contrary to the policy that Norway should pursue and that the Left stands for. I noticed a bit of a wretched response from Serigstad Valen to this challenge in a line from Line Henriette Hemdal here instead. The appropriations for cleaning up polluting masses have been reduced, which may lead to delays in cleaning. I'm sorry. The left is more satisfied with the government's priorities in the budget for nature protection. But the potential for forest protection is much higher. The Left proposes a number of increases in the allocations for key environmental and climate action such as research, environmental monitoring, wildlife stock conservation, cultural memory protection, open air action, forest protection, oil conservation, clean-up measures and environmental monitoring totaling 416,5 million. kr. In total, the Left's commitment to climate and environment measures contributes to a green tax reform that contributes to a total tax shift of about 8 billion. kr – about 2,7 billion. SEK in appropriations for 2011 and reserve provision for a total of SEK 5,2 billion. in addition to the Government's proposals. The left also proposes increasing the capital transfer to the research fund by a total of 70 billion. kr in a strategic focus on the knowledge and low-carbon society. That effort will be crucial to achieving the objective of the climate agreement.

These are local and central structures that have the task of thinking about environmental protection, and are one of the most important counter-forces that ensures that environmental protection actually takes place. Let me say a little bit about the climate summit in Cancún, which has been attended by several speakers. It was a thundering success, far beyond any expectation. It does not correspond to all the needs we have for climate action in the world, but it was an incredibly important step forward at a time when many people wondered if a UN process could deliver. There were five great successes in Cancún, as I see it, and let me quickly mention them: the first was that the meeting demonstrates that the UN can deliver on climate. We showed in Nagoya that we can deliver on natural diversity. Now it is demonstrated that you can deliver on climate, and it has recreated confidence and drive in the global multilateral process. Secondly, a broad agreement, such as the Cancún Agreement, will now lay a floor for progress in climate policy. We launched before Cancún the idea that it will not be the 'big bang' of climate policy, but we have to build stone on stone, floor by floor. What we have now done is to build substantial floors in the climate construction plant, and then the next agreement will go on from there. For a number of countries, the last year has been used to undermine what happened in Copenhagen. Many countries were not in Copenhagen. Those who were in Copenhagen did not even agree with what they had adopted. Now there's a foundation that everyone's behind, and we'll build on. Thirdly, there is an agreement on forest conservation, where Norway played a central role. We were the organizer of that process. Fourth, there is a green fund established, and fifthly, there is a significant leap forward in technology transfer from rich to poor countries. All in all, this was a strong tradition in Cancún in relation to what someone had expected, but even from a sober analysis we have to say that this was very, very good. The biggest reward goes to Mexico, which showed that a developing country — or at least an intermediate country — can operate international ultra-class diplomacy in a way that very few other countries would be able to do. Mexico's Foreign Minister, Patricia Espinosa, appeared as a global leadership at this conference, for which all of us should only bow in gratitude. Then there are two major weaknesses in the agreement that we have to address in the years to come. One is that we have now adopted a number of technology structures, green funds, rainforest conservation, etc., but they must be filled with money. It doesn't help to have paper structures if there's no money coming into them. The second and most important is that the emission functions contained in the Cancún agreement are not large enough. We must not forget that there are three times as many emission reductions in the Cancún agreement as in the Kyoto agreement. So when someone talks about the Kyoto agreement as a major success, in that case this is a significantly greater success. The Cancún agreement has much greater emission reductions than the Kyoto agreement, but it is not enough to achieve the global objective of preventing temperature rises above 2°C. In the future, the government will come back with a strategy towards Durban in 2011 for what Norway can contribute to making Durban a new floor in this building, and we will come back with the Norwegian measures in the Communication to the Stortinget in the autumn of 2011 on how to meet our part of the global climate objective.

Industrial power Mid-Norway has extended its concession. Unlike Industrikraft Møre in Elnesvågen, Industrikraft Mid-Norway in Skogn has no CO2 handling requirements. This week, agreement has been reached between Norway and Sweden on a green certificate market that aims to put in place 26 renewable TWh by 2020. Since a good part of this, after all, becomes wind power, the need for safe power availability from a stable supplier becomes even greater. A gas power plant in the Federal Republic can be such a stable factor. It is with interest that we record that Norwegian authorities and Statoil no profile that gas power can become Europe's climate change solution. This is fully in line with Sintef’s report which concludes that gas power in Norway, even without purification, will reduce European CO2 emissions. The government needs to take the situation in Central Norway seriously – gas power is an important and straightforward asset that will improve the situation for both business and private individuals.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e1802	That means that we continue to deal with case number. 2nd@item: inlistbox
ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e1805	First a little bit about the story – not that long. On 10 December last year, the Grand Duchy adopted a request to the Government to submit proposals for new legislation on pension schemes for depositors, members of the Government, judges of the Supreme Court, ombudsman and governor based on the guidelines drawn up in the Johnsen Committee's position, Document No. 19 for 2008-2009. Moreover, this sample, where all parties were represented, proposed new pension schemes for these groups based on the broad lines of the new social security scheme, also called modernised social security. The new arrangements were to be applied to new representatives and members of the government from the autumn of 2009 and to the Supreme Court judges, the Civil Ombudsman and the National Auditor appointed after 1 January 2011. The government's proposal does not at this stage include any proposal for new pension schemes for large-scale or state councils. The Ministry of Labour has previously announced its own proposal in spring 2011. I have to add that I then expect the government to deliver this proposition in such a good time that it will be possible to get a good and proper handling of the matter in the spring 2011 session. Nor does the proposal contain a bill on a pension scheme for a national auditor. Today, the salaries and pensions of the National Auditor are determined by the Presidency of the Grand Duchy. The majority of the Presidency proposes that the National Auditor's pension scheme be laid down in the National Audit Office Act, with the same adjustments proposed for the other specific pension schemes, i.e. its incorporation into the law and not the treatment of the presidency on a case-by-case basis. In the setting, two main types of legislative changes are proposed. Firstly, the specific pension schemes for Supreme Court Judges, Civil Ombudsman and National Auditor are terminated with effect for those appointed after 1 January 2011. In other words, new Supreme Court judges will be members of the State pension fund and have their pension calculated in accordance with the general rules on public service pension. So from then on, these groups will have the same vesting time and old-age pension method that applies to other government employees. Secondly, it proposes adaptations to the existing arrangements for the adopted changes in the social security system. This also applies to the representatives of the State and the State. These adjustments shall apply from 1 January 2011, as the main features of the comprehensive pension reform known to take effect. Both for the purpose of calculating pensions and in the interests of equality between the different groups, it is important that the adaptation to the new legislation enters into force at the same time. The adaptations consist in the introduction of life expectancy adjustments to the acquired rights — it is also, as is well known, a key element of modernised public health. This means that an individual's age pension is adjusted for changes in the average life expectancy of the population. If the overall life expectancy increases, this may lead to a lower annual age pension. An individual guarantee is therefore also introduced to ensure that the minimum payment is paid for what you have earned. Thus, for full-earned wholesalers within the existing scheme, a pension of 66 pst is guaranteed. of the compensation determined at the time of withdrawal of the old-age pension. New rules on pension regulation are also proposed. This is often referred to as indexation in the context of modernised public health. In all those special schemes, the pension is currently fixed as a certain percentage of the remuneration for the job or position in force at all times, i.e. if the remuneration of the depositors increases, the pension of retired representatives increases accordingly. So that's the current system I'm describing here. After the new system, pensions paid each year are to be regulated in line with the general wage growth in society, with a deduction of 0.75 p.p., as adopted for old-age pensions in the social security system and general public occupational pension schemes. The Ministry of Justice's legal department has assessed whether such conversion would be contrary to the provisions of the Basic Law § 97. For persons already receiving an old age pension, the Law Department concluded that the issue posed significant doubts and considered that it was uncertain what the conclusion of any judicial review would be. The law department was all the same. ‘reliable to assume that the amendments in question must be presumed to be in line with the non-return ban in the Grundgesetz § 97 in view of the serious societal considerations underlying the recent years of the review of pension systems and the unfortunate aspects of the assumption that the specific groups in question have a particularly protected position’. To put it another way, the Ministry of Justice believes that the Department of Law is able to take a stand on the following questions: Should our pensions be regulated according to particularly favourable rules in relation to popular rights, or should they not? That's what the constitutional question really applies to. It may also be argued that the grossing-up pension has a special position, because it is laid down by law how to regulate the pension scheme for grossing-up representatives. For the public's sake, this was supposed to happen through annual social security negotiations. One can discuss whether the term ‘negotiations’ really covers, but at least no annual regulation was laid down. So there's a certain difference, but the question is whether you want to process that difference to be to the favor of the populace at the expense of other groups. So I mean no, and there is a large majority in the presidency behind it. We also believe that a historical comparison between the annual wage growth of grossing-up representatives, judges, Supreme Court judges and the average wage growth in society shows that these groups do not have a basis for expecting a significantly higher wage trend than elsewhere in society. Nor shall it be disregarded that the new regulatory regime could involve a limited loss compared to the current system. However, a limited loss for smaller groups cannot have an impact on the implementation of general legislative reform. It has also been stated by the Supreme Court in connection with the plenary judgment in the case of the shipowner's cavalry, as it was known during that court's proceedings. For the sake of fundamental law, considerable emphasis must be placed on the fact that these groups have advanced positions in the Norwegian governance system, and that it would be unfortunate from a social point of view if we, including the members of the government, were to maintain a more favourable regulation of our occupational pensions than would apply to the rest of the population. This could undermine the acceptance of the necessary restructuring of the pension system and weaken the legitimacy of the entire pension reform. In addition, such special treatment of limited groups in key positions could weaken overall confidence in the management system — in policy, in a different way. On this basis, the majority of the presidency believes that the introduction of the new regulatory rules does not conflict with the Constitutional Act § 97, and I recommend the suspension on this basis.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e1837	We have now voted in favour of the postponement proposal we have promoted, and then it is only right for me to give it the signal that we are going to vote against the setting as it is available. ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e1994	We are currently processing the Annual Communications of the Data Protection Authority and the Data Protection Agency for 2009. As never before, privacy is being discussed. That is good, because it gives increased awareness of privacy as a fundamental right and ensures the digestion of the important work of the Data Protection Authority and the Subject. Technological developments challenge privacy: more information is stored and it is increasingly easier to search stored data. Protecting privacy does not mean turning the clock back. It's probably said from this pulpit before, but I'm saying it again: the Internet has come to stay. The benefits of being able to deliver the tax return electronically, apply for loans from the online bank, or use the Register of Cancer, to mention something, are great for each individual and at the same time bring great social benefits. For a number of good purposes, it is necessary to store and share personal data. However, any new intervention must be proportionate and based on an overall impact assessment. The Ministry warns against trying to resolve purely efficiency reasons and less pressing problems by putting privacy aside. It is an assessment to which an overall committee subscribes. The Data Storage Directive is mentioned in the Annual Report of the Data Protection Authority for 2009. The vision of the working party is known. We believe that the bill that came Friday represents a healthy balance between the interests of privacy and society's need for access to traffic data in the investigation and prosecution of serious crime. The Data Storage Directive represents both an intervention in and a strengthening of privacy. Network service providers are subject to a storage obligation, while at the same time the requirements for the provision of traffic data are strengthened from the current situation. In 2009, the Data Protection Authority carried out nearly 20 pst. more inspections than one year before. The control activities are an important contribution to the enforcement of the Data Protection Act and must be given priority. Unfortunately, the checks reveal extensive regulatory violations, and the Committee has noted in particular the concerns of the Data Protection Authority about non-compliance in smaller municipalities and small businesses. The wide international work of data protection is also of great importance. In particular, the EU is an important precursor of data protection standards and rules. It is therefore important that the European Data Protection Working Group has received observation status. Since 2005, the government has increased the operational budget of the Swedish Data Protection Authority by approximately 45 pst. It's given room for increased activity. In 2009, the Data Protection Authority established the guidance service to provide advice to persons who, for various reasons, wish to have personal data deleted on the Internet. Children and young people are a particularly vulnerable group. The annual report points to a new issue: that parents publish information about their children on the Internet in order to foster sympathy in their own child protection or child sharing cases. The Committee considers that there is a need for action in this area. Last year I took note of the importance of data protection acting in a way that strengthens confidence in supervisory judgement and independence. This year, too, we have unfortunately seen examples of the opposite. I would like to mention here the statements of the supervisory director at the time that it is only a matter of time before the police will demand that the contents of all telephone calls be stored. It's a claim it's not cover for. The opinion is only suitable for spreading unnecessary fear and undermining the confidence of the Data Protection Authority. The Committee takes note of the fact that the objective of the Data Protection Authority is to communicate positions in a clear manner, which are not at the expense of seriousness and backbone. Data subjects had more cases for treatment in 2009 than in previous years. Namely, the decision of the Data Protection Authority was re-examined in two of the 15 cases dealt with. The Committee has taken the view that the increase is not an expression of lack of procedure in the Data Protection Authority, but shows that several cases are case-by-case and complex. Finally, I would like to thank the Committee for its constructive contribution to the discussion of the matter. It is gratifying that there is broad agreement on the importance of data protection and broad disclosure as a fundamental right.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e2022	The Party of Progress notes that the Data Protection Authority is concerned that data protection is being weakened as a result of many individual measures that go beyond privacy and that data protection is set aside as a result of marginal, undocumented gains or purely efficiency considerations. In recent decades, there has been a huge technological development that one could expect to benefit only in a positive way, including in relation to privacy. However, in its annual report, the Data Protection Authority points out that this is the opposite, and in a large way. Every fifth municipality admits serious violations of security, but obtain help to secure privacy. Publication of case documents on the Internet, counterweights and forgotten prints – the list of privacy breaches is long in many Norwegian municipalities. A new investigation carried out by the Norwegian Information Security Centre and the Municipal Information Security Centre shows that Norwegian municipalities are poor at ensuring sensitive information. Small municipalities are often worse off than large ones. Data subjects received significant increases in the number of cases compared to previous years: 25 cases received in 2009 compared to six in 2008. The meaning of the large increase may be related to the supervisory object of the Data Protection Authority, as some businesses/objects view themselves to a greater extent than others tend to challenge the decisions of the Data Protection Authority. Despite the large amount of the case, the Privacy Policy has only converted two of the complaints. In the remaining final cases, they maintained their decision by the Data Protection Authority. ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e2037	The Data Protection Authority shall identify risks to privacy and shall provide us with advice on how to avoid these hazards and, where appropriate, limit the hazards. The annual communications to the Data Protection Supervisor and the Subject of Data Protection as a body of appeal give the impression of active and competent participation in supervision, advice and visible participation in the societal debate. We should be grateful for that. Nevertheless, there is a review tone in the notifications, and in particular in the Ministry's endorsement in the notification, that privacy is under constant pressure. You can get the impression that privacy is operated a bit from chance to chance. This impression of privacy on withdrawal is not based on feelings, but facts. A rich sample collection is found in the Data Protection Supervisor’s description of the controlled sectors. I'll draw attention to page 20, column 1: ‘Controls have shown an increasing tendency to store data on a larger scale and over longer periods of time than the regulatory framework provides. [[...]] [[...]] [[...]] The two controls that were terminated point towards a lack of respect for the rights granted to the data subject by the legislator.’ And in column 2: ‘Supervisory experience shows that the management tends to extend its own homes far, in some cases far beyond what is supported by the law.’ Further on page 21, column 2: ‘Many breaches of the regulatory requirements are detected. The level of seriousness varies, but there is far too little awareness and respect for the legal rules laid down in the Data Protection Act.’ The Data Protection Authority also stresses that the enterprises surveyed give the following impression: a general description is that the business has not become sufficiently familiar with the legislation, has far too little knowledge of who has to do what, has a poor overview of which personal data are processed, and has not reflected on the purposes and basis of processing. Furthermore, they have a lack of systematics in the approximation of legislation. Nor do they provide adequate safeguards for personal data, and they do not comply with key legal obligations, such as the obligation to provide information and erasure. There seems to be a significant distance between the regulation Stortinget adopts here in the house for the protection of privacy, and the reality in which establishments and businesses operate. In everyday life, the integrity of individuals is violated. One important lesson of this is that you have a solid distance from the outer boundaries of what you can accept, because you have to assume that there are errors in administration on a regular basis. Even within the social administration, which is established to secure people financially, and which should be close to taking account of individuals, we have just seen that Nav got his own home – actually better than the police – to get information about his clients' cell phone usage and actually used his home there for more than it was worth. One can reflect on why there are still exceedances of the quite far-reaching powers that the administration has caused us all to look into the cards. An explanation may be that privacy is not embedded as any central value in the sectored country. It is the sector interest to propose new ways of obtaining and using personal data. They have their own interests to care for and are often uncritical and sometimes unfit to assess whether the benefits of an intervention are real or proportionate. In particular, I am skeptical of the system where the sector interests are both taking the initiative for new data-invasive information gathering and at the same time assessing whether the intervention was proportionate. Thus, those who are intended to balance the fundamental and conflicting interests themselves must operate with the interest of the parties. The perceived benefits are often claimed to be concrete, while the disadvantages can be said to be shared among the broad public in general — i.e. on all of us. An important defence against privacy violations is, of course, that legislators have a realistic relationship to the fact that there will be administrative offences. It's actually possible to say no when some of the experts tell us that the world will collapse if not all technological opportunities are used to control what the population is doing. However, even in areas where the retention and processing of personal data has come to light, there is no doubt that social benefits do not violate the core of personal protection that can never be weighed down, there is reason to impose conditions, procedural requirements and sanctions for breaches of the data protection law. Here a more conscious legal policy is needed, and the responsibility for it lies in this Chamber.

We see a growing commitment to privacy, even though the «man in the street» is probably not concerned with privacy issues in everyday life. Several issues have helped raise privacy concerns. Among other things, the use of health information in research has received considerable attention in the press. However, the EU Data Storage Directive is in a particular class in terms of attention and debate. Rarely have so many opinions on privacy been as strong as in this case, and it is good that we have a comprehensive public debate on such important issues. Law proposition No. presented by the government 49 on the implementation of the Data Storage Directive in Norwegian law on 10 December, i.e. a few days ago. There is no doubt that the matter of implementing the Data Storage Directive has been a demanding matter for the government. Extensive storage of data from electronic communications undoubtedly represents a privacy breach. At the same time, it is important for the government to facilitate police use of the necessary tools to investigate serious crime. At the same time, when we propose that providers of electronic communications be required to store data for 12 months, it has been important to ensure strict rules on access to data in order to safeguard privacy. The threshold for police access to data has increased significantly in relation to the law in force. As a general rule, the criminal offence must have a four-year penalty framework. The provision of location data requires a five-year penalty framework. In addition, the data must be of material importance as evidence in the case. So it's not like the police can freely retrieve the data they want. Before data can be provided, a thorough assessment shall be carried out to balance, inter alia, the police’s need for data in the fight against crime. the privacy requirements of data subjects. Privacy must always be weighed against other social interests. And data protection assessments must be carried out early in the processes. Only in this way can they become an integral part of the decision-making basis. More and more, both nationally and internationally, talk about ‘privacy by design’, or built-in privacy, which appears to be the Norwegian concept. This means that privacy-friendly solutions are integrated into the technology as automatic first choice. It is a good way to get users to use privacy-friendly solutions. Using built-in privacy may include, for example: promoting the use of non-track alternatives and anonymous solutions, which are often difficult and costly if data protection assessments are to be submitted to the decision-making process. Identity theft is a phenomenon that has received increasing attention. Unfortunately, we have little information about the extent of the problem. But for those exposed to ID theft, it can be both very unpleasant and for some also have major economic consequences. The government is concerned that those closest to preventing ID theft must be conscious of their responsibility. Businesses themselves often have to ensure that solutions are in place to protect the safety of users and to prevent misuse of the identity of others. As part of the follow-up to the Commission’s report, FAD has launched two concrete actions followed up by the Data Protection Authority. The first is the deletion service, which can be used by people if they experience Internet abuses and want help to remove unwanted information. In addition, we are in the process of a project aimed at raising knowledge of privacy and ensuring better internal control and information security in Norwegian businesses. The Privacy Officers shall play an important role in this information and awareness-raising work. The Government is now working on a comprehensive follow-up to the challenges and proposals for action contained in the Commission’s report. It is important to look at complex data protection issues in the context so that we can best safeguard the individual’s right to privacy.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e2610	The size shall now address two issues relating to common standards and procedures for the return of third-country nationals without legal residence within the borders of the Schengen area. One case — Case No. 9 – applies to Norwegian accession to the so-called EU Return Directive. The Municipal Committee unanimously agrees with the Stortinget's agreement to accept the Directive and points out in a joint note the benefit of having common European rules on returns. It strengthens the legal certainty of third-country nationals residing in Europe. The suspension has been submitted to the Foreign Affairs and Defence Committee, which gives its assent without further comments. The second, case no. 8 – concerns the necessary changes to the Foreign Affairs Act, as a result of our adoption of the Directive. The Committee invited a hearing on 10 November 2010. No one signed up. To stop that no one has counter-conceptions, could have been tempting, but it might be to draw it well far. However, proposals for amendments to the Foreign Affairs Act as a result of the Directive have therefore been at the ordinary hearing of the Ministry of Justice, so that the possibility of making views and proposals is respected. The purpose of the Return Directive is important. It shall ensure a common humane and dignified return of people without legal residence in the Schengen Member State. In June this year, the Stortinget addressed a communication on Norwegian refugee and migration policy in a European perspective, in which we agreed to further work on common minimum standards for asylum and migration policy in Europe, based on international conventions. Then we also said we were willing to participate in the renegotiation of the Dublin Regulation in order to achieve a more solidarity in the distribution of responsibility for people in distress between the countries of Europe. Once we also have common minimum standards for their return without any legal delay, legal certainty for third-country nationals throughout the Schengen area is further strengthened. Minimum standards are minimum standards and do not prevent more liberal national rules on residence for human, humanitarian or other reasons. In a brief article, it is not possible to address all the provisions of the Return Directive that require amendments, or have already been observed in the Foreign Affairs Act, and I refer to Chapter 1 of Prop. 3 L, which gives a good overview of it. I believe that it is important to stress that being without a legal stay does not mean that people have done something wrong. It's e.g. the right to apply for asylum, but should be returned if the application is rejected. However, in some cases it is not that simple. Therefore, an important principle of legal certainty is that a decision on the return shall be taken after an individual assessment, taking into account more factors than the sole fact that the stay is illegal. The principle of protection against return shall be respected. The implementation of the Directive shall also take into account the best interests of children, family life and health. It is already considered covered by Norwegian legislation. But what's best for children is not always as obvious. In the preparatory work of the new Foreign Affairs Act, Stortinget has stated that the best way to give children greater weight is under the new Foreign Affairs Act than under the old one. The government has therefore started a work with a separate message about children in migration. There will be an important debate, because what we know is that children in such matters are always subject to the actions of adults – at good and bad. Among the most vulnerable are unaccompanied minor asylum seekers. It is therefore positive that the legal guarantee for this group is strengthened by a change in the law that forced return can only be made to a family member, an appointed guardian or a satisfactory return device. At the same time, it is important to ensure that voluntary return of lone minors is also carried out in a sound manner. The Directive is based on the fact that, as far as possible, returns should be made on a voluntary basis and that a time limit should be set for departure, but at the same time there are possibilities for the use of coercive agents if some try to avoid return. A new provision lists the factors to be taken into account when deciding whether there is a risk of avoidance. It strengthens the legal certainty of individuals and makes such decisions more predictable. The Committee's position is unanimous, except for a few points where the Advance Party wants a stronger intervention against individuals than the other parties do. They oppose a proposal to reduce the minimum time for refusal of entry to one year. They want an increased period of imprisonment for those who do not cooperate on return, but I assume that the Progress Party itself will present its views on the matter and address its proposal. Otherwise, the Committee's position is unanimous.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e2629	It may seem that we have been discussing the EU directive all day, but this is actually the first EU directive to be dealt with in the Stortingen today – and, to a certain extent, a very important one as well. The Progress Party has long called for a policy in this area that is consistent in many ways with the changes made to the Foreign Affairs Act in implementing the Return Directive. We are in a unanimous committee, but we have, as the rapporteur referred to, proposals on which we stand alone. We are going against a change to Section 71, which is quite correct about the minimum time of the entry ban. We want to keep today's wording in the Foreign Affairs Act in two years' time, while the law now adds up to one year. Furthermore, we are promoting proposals for a new third paragraph of Section 106, which is in line with the government's proposal for the third sentence of the third paragraph, which states that we do not want the 12 week limitation of the government's proposal. We want to make greater use of imprisonment for up to 18 months, which is maximum under the Return Directive. I note that a great deal of what is contained in the Return Directive is in many ways a policy that the Progress Party has put forward and stood for earlier, particularly in connection with the adoption of a new Foreign Law by the Stortinge in the previous period. The implementation of the Return Directive implies the adoption of a decision on expulsion with a future entry ban in more cases than at present against foreigners who are illegally resident. Accordingly, following the Directive and the legislative proposal, a decision on expulsion must be taken if the foreigner is not granted a period of voluntary return or the foreigner does not comply with the exit period. The Ministry has then estimated that the Directive will lead to about 750 more expulsion decisions each year. So it has consequences. The Ministry has assessed the rules on imprisonment under the Foreign Affairs Act in relation to the Directive. After the Directive, imprisonment can only take place for a period longer than six months if the foreigner does not cooperate in the posting or there are delays in obtaining the necessary documents from the authorities of another country. The rule under the Foreign Affairs Act today is that imprisonment should not take place for more than 12 weeks unless there are specific reasons. It will primarily be situations where non-residents do not cooperate in return or where the delay is caused by the home Member State authorities that there will be more than 12 weeks of imprisonment today. The Ministry has therefore proposed to maintain the general rule that imprisonment should not take place for more than 12 weeks in total, rather than extending the prison period to six months, even if the directive as such would have allowed it to do so. Furthermore, in line with the Directive’s requirements, the Ministry has proposed that, even in cases where it can be detained for a period longer than 12 weeks, no longer than 18 months, which is the maximum period after the Directive. I would like to stress that the proposal of the Progress Party that there should be no limit to the prison period other than the maximum limit of 18 months in this way is directly contrary to the Directive. I would also like to stress that the clarification contained in the legislative proposal on what elements may be particularly relevant when assessing whether there is a risk of avoidance both reinforces the legal certainty of the foreigner by providing guidance on the content of the assessment, and at the same time makes it clear that, among other things, it does. are entitled to pay attention to whether the foreigner has been penalised or is responsible for serious disturbances of the order of the person receiving asylum.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e2840	In this proposition, the government has presented proposals for changes to the Law on Social Security and some other laws. The substantive changes in the Law on Social Security are mainly linked to the 2011 draft budget and to the introduction of a new age pension. In addition, some legislative adjustments, clarifications and corrections are also proposed in the Swedish Social Security Act, the Labour Market Act and the Social Welfare Administration Act. In order to strengthen women’s rights to daily allowances, the Government proposes to take maternity benefits, maternity-related sickness benefits and parental benefits into the qualifying basis for entitlement to daily benefits. The amendment strengthens female workers in pregnancy and childbirth. The Government proposes to repeal special rules for day-care recipients aged 64 or more. The abolition of special rules means that the legislation on day-to-day pay for the elderly is similar to that for the other beneficiaries of the day-to-day pay. In order to ensure predictability for beneficiaries already affected by the specific rules, the Government proposes a transitional period of one year. The Ministry of Labour will ask the Directorate of Labour and Welfare to follow the unemployed in this group in particular, as many have said in this Chamber earlier, with a view to helping those who need it to get back to work. The introduction of a flexible old-age pension into the social security system from 62 years of age gives freedom to combine labour and pension income without any curtailment. In the proposal, the government proposes that the daily allowance should not be coordinated against the old age pension and the new AFP in the private sector and that the redundancy allowance should not be coordinated against the old age pension. The new old-age pension also means that it is necessary to make adjustments to the health insurance legislation from 1 January 2011 for persons over 62 years of age. The Government proposes that persons between the age of 62 and 67 continue to enjoy full health insurance rights as at present. Persons between 67 and 70 years of age with an income of over 2 G receive a limited entitlement to sickness benefit for up to 60 days regardless of whether or not an old-age pension has been taken out. A minority opposes this last proposal and believes that a higher cost is shifted to employers who pay full wages under illness. It is true that people between 62 and 70 years of age who do not take out an old age pension have full health insurance rights today. In practice, however, few or none will benefit from not withdrawing an old age pension at 67 years after today’s rules. Pension withdrawals also do not prevent you from earning a higher pension up to 70 years. As a result, there is nothing to lose on taking out an old-age pension, and for most people sick pay up to 60 days next to a full old-age pension will give you a higher total payment than just sick pay. The proposal also proposes that the period during which a person may receive a redundancy allowance without fulfilling the requirement that the working capacity be reduced by at least half or the requirement that a person be in need of treatment, work-oriented measures or other follow-up from the seat of employment and welfare should be limited to six months. Persons who, after the six months, need assistance to get into work will be able to receive redundancy payments according to the general rule.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e2880	Both the seafarers’ organisations and the Fiskers’ team are very pleased that today the Storting makes changes to the pension scheme for seafarers and fishermen. Pension schemes are now adapted to the pension reform. Neither the seafarer's pension nor the fisherman's pension shall be adjusted or coordinated. Seafarers' pension is an early retirement for the age group from 60 to 66 years. It includes Norwegian citizens and persons permanently resident in Norway who have been employed on Norwegian ships and mobile devices above 100 gross tonnes, registered in Norwegian registers. At the end of the year, the pension scheme included approximately 28 000 seafarers. Fishermen's pension includes all fishermen who are registered on page B of the fishing manual, which means that they have fishing and fishing as full-time workers. The fishing pension is an early retirement for fishermen aged 60 to 66 years and includes about 10 200 fishermen. In the introduction of a new pension scheme from 1 January 2011, mariners’ and fishermen’s pensions could be provided in full alongside old-age pensions when they are taken out before the age of 67, i.e. no coordination. However, current coordination rules apply to all those who have withdrawn the AFP before 1 January 2011 and to the AFP in the public sector. Seafarers’ and fishermen’s pensions are not coordinated with new AFPs in the private sector. Seafarer’s pension and fishing pension shall be regulated annually as most other statutory pension schemes, i.e. with the wage growth of society less than 0,75 pst. Although this results in a lower level of purchasing power relative to the wage growth of the economically active, both seafarers and fishermen will be ensured a good and stable purchasing power development, in line with other pensioners. As part of the seafarer’s pension scheme, a transitional allowance is provided to old-age pensioners in cases where the seafarer’s pension is higher than the old-age pension. The transitional allowance corresponds to the difference between a seafarer’s pension and an old-age pension. At present, the Storting imposes a claim for a loss of income for redundancy payments and invalidity pensions. I don't think the concept of loss of income is found in Norwegian dictionaries. This means, however, that people who have reached the age of 62 must have lost income in the order of the basic amount of the population’s wealth, i.e. about 75 000 DKK in the last year, in order to seek redundancy benefits and disability benefits. This means that up to 60 people could lose the right to work clearance, and up to 300 people could lose the right to disability benefit. To the extent that this will help more people increase their job fractions and stand longer in position, it will strengthen the working line. However, to the extent that this leads to the fact that someone in small positions without full rights in old age is forced into a long-term low age pension, it is unfortunate. I therefore do not underestimate the concerns associated with this proposal. ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e2923	The rapporteur has given a good account of the content of the proposal presented here today. I have noticed that the representative, like the chairman of the committee, has noticed that there has been a short time to deal with this proposition. After all, the work on pension reform has been going on for several years and is based on broad political settlements in the United Kingdom. The reform contributes to ensuring a sustainable pension system in the future as well, and broad support for the reform is important. The pension system is an important pillar of welfare in the country and changes in the pension system will have an effect on many for a long time in the future. From 1 January, flexible withdrawals of old-age pensions will be possible for those over 62 years of age. Pensions are adjusted by their standard of living and the annual regulation of pensions is detached from the basic amount of the population. The pensioners will continue to be guaranteed a good purchasing power. The new rules of social security require adaptations to the early retirement schemes for seafarers and fishermen and to the supplementary benefit scheme. With this proposal, a seafarer's pension and a fisherman's pension may be granted in addition to an old-age pension from the age of 62 to 67 years. In addition, a seafarer's pension and a fisherman's pension during payment shall be regulated as an old-age pension in social security. It is also announced that a wider review of the arrangements will be carried out with a view to finding lasting adaptations to the new rules in the area of public health. In the supplementary social security scheme, the beneficiaries of the social security scheme shall be guaranteed the minimum level of social security. In the new pension scheme, the special supplement and the minimum pension are not continued. The minimum benefit will be a defined minimum pension level and the measurement of supplementary benefit must be linked to this. It is also proposed to regulate supplementary social benefits corresponding to the minimum level of pensions. I have noticed that the Advance Party is going in order that additional social benefits continue to be regulated in line with the basic amount. The result would be that today's minimum-pension old-age pensioners would gradually switch to supplementary social benefits, and that would be pointless. As with the introduction of the scheme, additional social benefits should continue to be equal to the minimum pension level in the social security system. As indicated in the budget, it is proposed to introduce a requirement for a loss of income for the right to work clearance and disability pension for persons over 62 years of age. However, it is proposed that the requirement should not apply if the member does not receive benefits as a surviving spouse at the minimum pension level or is not entitled to full retirement pension. In addition, some proposals of a more technical nature have been made. I am pleased that the majority of the committee supports the proposals that are under consideration today. This proposal will take an important step forward with adjustments in the pension system to a new age pension from social security.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e3102	The Christian People's Party wants to improve the basic framework conditions for value creation, so that Norway can also offer good welfare services to its inhabitants in the future. In this context, the conditions for smaller enterprises are very central. We will pursue an economic policy that promotes innovation and industrial development and provides predictability for those who want to invest in jobs. In the document we are discussing today, the Christian People's Party proposes a number of measures to improve the conditions of small businesses through measures to facilitate working life, better conditions for value creation, simpler everyday life and better interaction between the public sector and private business. As the fleet representative mentioned, several of the proposals are supported by the other opposition parties, while, unfortunately, the government parties will not give priority to measures to ensure this value creation in small enterprises. Small businesses account for more than half of the value creation in Norwegian business. Small businesses are innovative and adaptable, but it is demanding for them to survive and grow. Five years after start-up is only 30 pst. by the enterprises still active. This is the background to the ‘Small Business Call’, which has four main requirements for governments: less square work, smarter taxation, simpler everyday life and smarter interaction between public and business. The Christian People's Party believes that the government should improve the conditions for small businesses, in order to ensure value creation, jobs and well-being in the future. It is necessary to stimulate more enterprises to become apprentices. Support for training offices and apprenticeships needs to be increased to make it easier for smaller enterprises to involve more apprentices. This makes it easier for younger people to enter the labour market and improves access to skills for businesses. The Christian People's Party believes that public purchasers should take into account whether companies have apprentices when deciding who is to be offered. It will give enterprises with apprentices a competitive advantage. Norway must appear as an attractive country for investment in new businesses and have a targeted policy to give entrepreneurs good conditions. In addition to ensuring risk capital through the public instrument, private investors wishing to invest in start-ups need to be guaranteed framework conditions that increase access to risk capital. Three-quarters of Norwegian enterprises are owned by families. 40,000 enterprises are facing a generational shift over the next 10 years in this country, and surveys by the European Commission show that a successful change of ownership in small enterprises leads to an average of five jobs being continued. The Christian People's Party therefore proposes that the payment of the inheritance fee be postponed for at least 10 years in the event of a generation change. If the holding continues to operate, one tenth of the fee shall be cancelled each year. If the holding continues to operate for at least 10 years, no inheritance tax will be paid. This will be good business policy, good social economy and good district policy. Most owners of small enterprises work in their own businesses, and the vast majority of small enterprises in Norway are owned by private individuals. The Christian People's Party believes that it is particularly unfortunate that owners have to pay property taxes even when businesses go through with deficits, which is very demanding for smaller businesses. The Norwegian public procurement market has grown dramatically in the last ten years. The public spends about 400 billion each year. NOK for the purchase of goods and services. Good public procurement makes better use of society's resources. Cooperation with private businesses has created new and smarter solutions in several areas of society. Experience from other countries shows that it is realistic to believe that a total of 40 billion. Public procurement can be directly linked to innovation. Here the smaller companies have great opportunities to participate and compete for the deliveries. The Christian People's Party therefore asks the government to ensure that public procurement down to NOK 100 000 is published electronically. 99 pst. by all enterprises in Norway are small or medium-sized enterprises. Better conditions for value creation, simplification, better interaction between public and business and a simpler working life are essential to increase value creation. I refer to the proposal, which is in the vote.

So an alternative to introducing a separate minimum deduction for sea-going traders is that the deduction of persons is greatly increased, so that one thus receives a more equal treatment between sea-going traders and employees. With regard to the issue of two share laws, there are proposals that are interesting to discuss further, just as the government is now discussing the issue of the removal of audit obligations for small limited liability companies. My experience from the Ministry of Local Affairs is that many in the business community want complicated rules to have some jobs. It's pretty complicated. Finally, we are happy with the theme, we want better terms, we want to work systematically and thoroughly with each of them, because this becomes more important in the future, when the economic situation gets worse around us.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e3368	Prop. 5 L for 2010–2011 deals with amendments to Law No 3 June 1994. 15 on the Entity Register, i.e. the unitary register law, and the law of 21 June 1985 no. 78 on the registration of undertakings, i.e. the company register law. It is a unanimous committee that makes a recommendation and that otherwise has no dissenting comments but refers to the proposition. However, it must be said that the parties of government on one side and the Party of Advancement, the Right and Christian parties on the other have their own comments. As regards the case itself, the Committee has noted that the proposal for amendments to the law involves an extension of the scope for whom a written declaration can be made on behalf of the undertaking concerning information notified to the Brønnøysund Registers. Furthermore, the legislative amendments provide for a relaxation in the signing requirements for declarations and reporting to the registers, if the information to be provided is provided through Altin. The current signature rules and strict signature requirements have been largely continued without major changes since the law of 17 May 1890 – and that is a few years ago. Furthermore, the Committee has noted that, according to the Brønnøysund Registers, the experience is that electronic notifications have a lower error rate compared to submissions that need to be submitted manually on the basis of paper versions. According to the registers, this is due to built-in checks in the electronic form solutions. Otherwise, the Committee notes that the legislative changes promoted in the proposal could result in significant administrative and financial savings for businesses by allowing more people to avail themselves of the possibility of submitting information electronically instead of in paper form. It is otherwise a fixed objective to 95 pst. registrations to the Brønn Island Health Registers shall be possible by electronic means. Such an objective could, over time, result in potential efficiency gains for the Entity Registry and the Business Register of approx. eight years' work. With this I present the committee's recommendation.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e3389	As the case spokesman said, the main point of this case is to be reconciled. It may be worth noting that we have finished after the previous debate. The amendment proposal is still part of the government's simplification work. Electronic transmission of messages to Brønnøsundregistra simplifies the everyday life of business operators, and it reduces the processing time by registering materially. This is actually a real improvement and simplification. The aim is that the percentage of electronic messages should be 95 before the end of 2012. As we all see, there's a little bit of time until we've reached it. The current regulatory authority will be extended to cover all types of messages and all those subject to a notification obligation can sign separately. If necessary, a delegate can use the Altinn admin rights to a natural person. The circle of persons required to report in the Device Register is expanded. In order to have uniform rules in the Registration Code, the circuit that can sign both paper messages and electronic messages to the Device Register is also extended accordingly. This is good. When you read the comments of the Right Party and the Advance Party, you can be sure that it is impossible to do business in Norway. I'm not going to go up the debate that we had in the previous debate, but I'm still going to point out that the fact that we're ready for the eighth best country to operate in hasn't brought everyone. It's unique that while we're getting that crew, we're also ready for the world's best country to live in, going on and on.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e3402	The government has determined that electronic communications should be the primary channel of contact with the public sector. Electronic services make communication between enterprises, individuals and the public simpler and less resource-intensive. Facilitating easier electronic communication with the public is thus an important contribution to business simplification. The aim is that electronic services should fully replace paper in the communication between industry and the public sector. The authorities have therefore worked actively for many years to develop good electronic solutions for business. The proposal is part of the government's simplification work. The laws, regulations and administrative provisions shall not create unnecessary administrative costs for the enterprises. The authorities thus contribute to the release of time by businesses. The proposal presented in the proposal implies that the rules on who is to sign messages to the Unit Registry and the Business Register are simplified, in particular by reducing the number of persons required to sign the messages. The entity register and the Business Register contain central information about Norwegian companies and is an important source of information for both public authorities and private parties. Both registers offer online registration of most enterprises and all data submitted are stored electronically. Since December 2006, companies have been able to submit messages electronically with a signing solution via Altinn. The electronic share is now at approx. 45 pst. The aim is, as the rapporteur said, that it should be 95 p.m. by 2012. The rules on who is obliged to ensure that messages are sent and who is to sign the notification itself are detailed in the entity register law and the company register law. Signing implies that the information being notified is correct. For most forms of organisation, more people have to sign the message. The feedback from business users is that today’s requirements for signing in the Unit Register Act and the Business Register Act are a barrier to the use of electronic transmission. The proposal implies that all operators currently subject to notification obligations to the Brønn Island Health Registers can sign the notification on their own. Regulations will also provide that the right to sign the notification may be delegated by electronic means to a natural person whom the entity itself designates by means of the administrator’s rights in Altin. The proposed change implies that the threshold for using electronic reporting is lowered. For enterprises, it also means simplification that they can identify themselves the person to sign messages on behalf of the enterprise. There are clear simplification effects for business by submitting messages electronically rather than on paper. Figures obtained from a report ordered by the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade in October 2009 show that during the period 2006–2009, the business sector saved approx. Eighty million. kr by increasing the use of electronic messages from 10 to 24 pst. An increase from today's 45 pst. electronic messages for 95 pst. means that the number of electronic messages to the registers is increased by 200000. This could lead to significant savings for business. Increased electronic notification revenue provides for more effective procedures in the Brønn Island Health Registers. Calculations show that an electronic message income of 95 pst. could provide potential savings in several annual works, which could instead be used to increase efforts in other areas. Increased use and better quality of registers will bring benefits to business and other users. The proposed changes are one in themselves modest, but equally important, part of the government's efforts to simplify and facilitate business. Happy is the fact that, at the same time, we are creating a more efficient and user-friendly public sector. Finally, I would like to say that I am pleased that the Committee has given its unique positive attitude to these legislative changes, which will both increase the use of electronic messages to the registers and streamline and improve the quality of registers, while at the same time implying simplification and resource savings for business.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e3450	Let me first take the opportunity to thank the Committee for a good cooperation in order to get this matter expeditiously up to the Grand Duchy and its proceedings. It has been of great benefit to me as the case rapporteur in this case. The proposal on the changes currently in the law on coverage is the last element to complete the guarantee scheme on long-term power contracts for the power-generating industry. The aim of the scheme is to make it easier for industry to obtain long-term financing when concluding long-term power contracts. The scheme could contribute to increasing liquidity in the market for long-term power contracts, and in such a case would be an important contribution to ensuring the long-term framework conditions for the power-intensive industry, which can once again develop its activities in Norway. The legislative proposal we are discussing now involves a change in the law on coverage, which means that contracts for the right of entry into long-term power contracts take precedence over the residence of the power buyer, subsequent creditors and subsequent legal formations in the power buyer's contractual position. The background to the bill is that under the law in force, there is uncertainty as to whether an agreement on the right of entry into force of power agreements would be contrary to the right of entry into bankruptcy of the bankruptcy residence. Under the law in force, it is also uncertain whether the long-term energy contracts desired to be concluded in industry can be pledged. It was therefore important to bring this change of law into place now, so that the scheme would be complete. Throughout this decade, power to industry has been a topical political issue. There has been a long time to find a solution that would remedy the situation following the termination of State contracts as a result of State aid law. A relief system has therefore been requested. In this way, the scheme presented by the government on 14 September 2010 was well received. Norwegian Industry characterised in an online message on the same day that the guarantee system was known, that it was happy that the guarantee system has now come into place, and that it was now up to the power companies and the industry to negotiate. Power at competitive and stable prices is an important prerequisite, they further stated, for the development of the power-generating industry in this country. Of the 80-90 companies currently covered by the scheme, we know that there are more people who want and need to enter into new long-term power agreements in the coming years. This is linked to the expiry of existing power agreements and many plans for new investments. Investments in the power-intensive industry have a long horizon. This means that the framework conditions must also be long-term and also the power agreements, which must be considered essential for attracting new investment. Through the guarantee mechanism now completed by this amendment, the parties have an instrument to manage at least the counterparty risk associated with long-term power agreements. This should mean that, when the scheme is in place, there are greater possibilities for achieving long-term power contracts than it was in the past. But at the same time, I would like to stress that, in my opinion, the power seller has a special responsibility to enter into long-term power contracts with the industry. At the same time, however, industry has to take on its own responsibility for setting up such agreements, and much has now been put in place. If the power-processing industry is phased out, there will be major changes in national power consumption. At my discretion, this is extremely unfortunate and not desirable. Therefore, the parties now have a responsibility to use this tool to try to find long-term solutions with which both parties can live. I would like to stress that we now have expectations that the industry, when it now – hopefully – enters into power agreements using the State guarantee scheme, is offered conditions reflecting that the guarantee scheme provides a significant reduction in the seller’s risk by entering into long-term contracts. It should also be expected that long-term contracts and any advance payments will give sellers greater freedom to realise their investment plans by strengthening the liquidity of power sellers by advance payments. It should also be motivating. I hope that in the time that is coming, we can see that this is reflected through power agreements that are being concluded. I am therefore very pleased that we can now complete the scheme and, as it is already late in the evening, I will refrain from commenting on the minority's comments in this case.
