ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e3522	I am pleased that a Joint Liaison Committee supports the proposal to provide legal protection for agreements on the right of entry into long-term power contracts. The amendment of the law will give guarantors in long-term power contracts the right of entry before the bankruptcy dwelling in a bankruptcy situation. The proposal is an important element for the guarantee system for power-intensive industry purchasing to be an effective instrument. The proposal will make it easier for the power-intensive industry to obtain funding at the time of the conclusion of long-term power contracts. On 8 December 2009, the majority adopted the government’s proposal to establish a guarantee scheme for power-intensive industry’s purchase of power. The scheme shall be managed by the Export Credit Guarantee Institute and provided a framework of 20 billion. kr. The Government has proposed to continue the framework of the 2011 budget. The guarantee scheme shall apply to power contracts of a minimum duration of seven years and a maximum of 25 years. I believe that the guarantee scheme will contribute to new investments and secure jobs in the Norwegian power-intensive industry. The Government proposes that a new provision be included in the law on coverage. This provision will provide legal protection for contracts that give guarantors the right of entry into long-term power contracts. Thus, such agreements would go ahead of the bankruptcy estate’s right under Section 7-3 of the Law on Coverage to enter into the debtor’s agreements in the event of bankruptcy. This helps to reduce the risk of guarantors in individual guarantee exposures and can therefore be crucial for the conclusion of guarantee agreements in the scheme. The Government's proposal includes the right of entry into force of power agreements with a term of seven years or more. The aim of the guarantee scheme and the legislative proposal is to make it easier for industry to obtain finance in the conclusion of long-term power contracts. This could lead to increased liquidity in the long-term power contracts market and will therefore make an important contribution to ensuring that the power-intensive industry further develops its activities in Norway. The Government proposes that the guarantor be obliged, by means of an agreement on the right of entry, to give up the value added in the power agreement to the buyer, the buyer's dwelling or holders of other rights in the contract position. The proposal takes account of the role of the bankruptcy dwelling in the restructuring of businesses, including the desire to ensure the continued operation of industrial activities. In this way, the need for security and the interests of the guarantor are well balanced. The guarantee scheme shall apply to establishments registered within the SN2002, 20, 21, 24 and 27. This includes activities in wood and wood products, wood processing, chemical products and metals. However, the current statistical classification of economic activities changes from time to time. Both the Statistical Central Bureau and the Register of Units in the Brønnøsund Registers have now used SN2007 as a substitute for SN2002. I propose that a provision be made for the coverage law § 7-14 on the legal protection of contracts for the right of entry into long-term power contracts that regulates the transition from the food codes in SN2002 to SN2007. The business codes that will apply in the regulation of the coverage law § 7-14 will, of course, also apply to the guarantee scheme. I have noticed that the minority in the Business Committee, the members of the Advance Party, the Right Party and the Christian People's Party, believe that the government has not delivered in terms of giving industry safe access to power at acceptable prices. I do not agree with that. I believe that the government has provided comprehensive measures to strengthen industry's power supply. I have already mentioned the guarantee scheme. Several measures have also been put in place to ensure the balance of forces and the development of new forces. During the period 2006-2009, about 3.5 TWh of wind and hydropower production and a concession of about 6 TWh have been granted. There are also plans for further development, including: through renewable energy deployment through a common electricity certificate market with Sweden. The launch of a single electricity certificate market for Norway and Sweden from 1 January 2012 will result in a large-scale deployment of renewable energy until 2020. Overall, the new electricity certification system will provide 26.4 TWh from 2012 to 2020, broken down by 13.2 TWh in each country. There is a production corresponding to about eight large gas power plants. Support is provided for energy efficiency improvement in industry. Since 2001, Enova has concluded contracts for nearly 14 TWh in the field of renewable energy and energy efficiency improvement. One third of this has been achieved through Enova’s industry-oriented programmes. In total, industry has received support of about 1 billion. kr. Under the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade, an aid scheme is in place for enterprises in consortia of purchases. It was already in place in the context of the 2009 budget and is managed by Innovation Norway. I would also like to remind you that the government in the re-balancing of the State budget for 2010 has made proposals to strengthen state capital by a total of 14 billion. kr. The objective is to facilitate the realisation by State power of an investment plan of approximately 82 billion. kr, enabling the company to implement forward-looking investments in hydro and wind power and district heating. This government has been committed to providing industry with safe access to power at acceptable prices. I think I have a total mass behind me when I say that this is an area that needs to be of high priority. The support of a Joint Liaison Committee for this legislative proposal is a significant contribution to this work.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e3938	The Norwegian welfare model is based on good and close cooperation with the voluntary sector. Better framework conditions through increased appropriations and greater recognition of volunteering have been at the heart of our government's voluntary policy. The non-profit sector is a major and important provider of healthcare services. The public sector is therefore dependent on the provision of quality services at a reasonable price by the non-profit sector. On the other hand, the purchase of such services by the State and local authorities is also of great importance for the non-profit sector. The public must act in a professional manner and in accordance with the rules governing public acquisitions. At the same time, we must ensure that the ideal organisations have stable and good framework conditions. Today, we have an exception to the procurement legislation when purchasing health and care services from the non-profit sector. Public procurement of health and care services from NPISHs does not need to follow the detailed procedural rules. However, the contracting entity must comply with the essential requirements of the legislation, including: that the purchase is, as far as possible, based on competition. This results from the law on public acquisitions and our international obligations under the EEA Agreement. It is not possible to conclude agreements with non-profit actors in direct negotiations. So today's legislation gives a choice to the buyer of a service: either you can hold restricted competitions, where only NPISHs are offered, or you can conduct competitions in accordance with the procedural rules of the procurement law. The public must choose the most appropriate approach in the specific situation. In addition, professional action must be taken to ensure that health and care services maintain a good quality at a reasonable price. At the same time, it is important to ensure good and proper procurement through clear purchasing strategies. The Government is concerned that the legislative space for action is being used to ensure long-term and predictable framework conditions for actors. It is worth considering whether contracts have an appropriate length in any case. Consideration of the necessary investments to be made by operators and the need for stability and continuity of users must be central to the choice of contract duration. At the same time, I find it difficult to see that it is possible to establish some general guidelines on the length of the contract that apply in all cases – regardless of the services to be acquired. This must necessarily depend on a concrete assessment based, inter alia, on: what to acquire and how complex the services are. The needs of users and the quality of services must be central to the choice of procurement procedures. The competition requirement does not necessarily require suppliers to compete primarily on price. The regulatory framework allows for heavy involvement of quality criteria at all stages of competition. At the same time, the public sector — including in the purchase of services — shall use the resources in the most efficient and efficient way possible. Money spent on inefficient procurement could alternatively have been used to achieve other welfare objectives and to finance other welfare services. The Government is concerned that employee rights are well respected in the procurement procedures. Regulation on pay and working conditions in public contracts is an important instrument and the government is working to improve this legislation. The Minister of Health has already – and there is a lot of debate here on health and care services – announced that work will be started on a general review of the framework conditions for the non-profit sector. I have joined this. The government will come back to Stortinget in an appropriate manner with a detailed explanation. Stopping all new tenders requires the extension of existing contracts. A new contract will be considered unless the extension is part of the original competition. Renewal without a new call will be illegal. The alternative is for users to stand without services. I cannot therefore see that it is neither appropriate nor that we have legal access to stop all tendering procedures.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e4165	I heard the red-green parties say in connection with a similar position on 9 June this year that it was also urgent. It is because a government now has a government that is in principle opposed to offers on routes, but allows health businesses and Bufetat to submit bids with blindfolds for children, children's protection, intoxication, psychiatry and rehabilitation. I will explain to the representative Aasland what a bid for blindfolds is. It's e.g. where a health undertaking authorises one major operator to submit a tender. Next time, no one else can take part in that competition, all the little ones are wiped out. One also has no option as a patient to choose between different offers. Tenders are available for the eyes, because one could make it clear in the tender that a person would like to take care of a variety of offers and had the opportunity to do so. Tenders for the eyes are tendered when one says that quality requirements should apply to 70 pst., price 30 pst. When such tenders are submitted, one defines that all the operators comply with the quality requirements. So they're all equivalent in terms of the 70 psts. Then it's only the 30 pst. on price which means something, i.e. no quality requirements. Tenders for the eyes are offered when, for example: states that all drug institutions should be able to receive LAR patients. That means those drug institutions which, in principle and professionally, believe that it is wrong to mix drug addicts who are drug-free, and drug addicts who are drug addicts from the state. They are then defined out of the tender immediately – i.e. the variety disappears. Tenders are offered for the eyes when you have a call for tenders within the psychiatry where you do not define in advance what offers you have, such as: Oslo Hospital obviously loses a tender because they offer to mentally ill people who need a completely different follow-up — in other words, a completely different manning from that required by the other institutions participating in that tender, and thus loses the offer on price, because Health South-East has made a binding bid for the eyes. The representatives of the government parties say that the note says it – the note says it. Yes, but on the part of the government, there is absolutely nothing going on, because both Bufetat and, in particular, regional health companies are allowed to do that practice. They've been doing that for five years, and the consequences are dramatic.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-12-13.ana.ud2069e12939	Six proposals have been put forward during the debate. It's the proposals No 1 and 2, by Elisabeth Røbekk Nørve, on behalf of the Party of Advancement and Right-wing Proposals No. 3-6, from Rigmor Andersen Eide on behalf of the Christian People's Party It is first voted on the proposals of the Christian People's Party. Proposal No. 3 sounds: «The decision requests the Government to put a full invitation to tender immediately. All on-going procurement processes are frozen until new guidelines are in place.» Proposal No. 4 sounds: ‘The Government is asked to ensure that all non-profit actors are lifted out of the bidding rounds. Agreements with non-profit actors shall be concluded by direct negotiation of contracts instead of by competitive tendering. For the acquisition of health and social services from non-profit institutions serving households, direct negotiations on operating contracts are used, not tendering procedures.» Proposal No. 5 sounds: ‘The government is asking to ensure that the ideal actors in the health and social sector, due to their special character, role and specific situation, have the same type of ongoing contracts as the major non-profit hospitals do.’ Proposal No. 6 sounds: ‘The government asks the government to ensure that the challenges surrounding the pension obligations of non-profit actors are addressed’.
